wary about using them to justify capitalizing these expenses. For an
operating expense to be capitalized there should be substantial evidence
that the benefits from the expense accrue over multiple periods. Does a
customer who is enticed to buy from Amazon (AMZN), based upon an
advertisement or promotion, continue as a customer for the long term?
There are some analysts who claim that this is indeed the case and
attribute significant value added to each new customer. It would be
logical, under those circumstances, to capitalize these expenses using a
procedure similar to that used to capitalize R&D expenses.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre19">

> > <span class="calibre17">1. Determine the period over which the
> > benefits from the operating expense (such as SG&A) will flow.</span>
>
> > <span class="calibre17">2. Estimate the value of the asset (similar
> > to the research asset) created by these expenses. This amount will
> > be added to the book value of equity/capital and used to estimate
> > the returns on equity and capital.</span>
>
> > <span class="calibre17">3. Adjust the operating income for the
> > expense and the amortization of the created asset.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre55" data-border="1">

> <div class="calibre29">
>
> > <span class="calibre6"><span class="bold">Value Driver \#2:
> > Efficiency of Intangible Investments</span></span>
>
> <div class="calibre25">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> > <span class="calibre30">Not all investments in intangible assets
> > create value. In your firm, how quickly do investments in intangible
> > assets pay off as profits? How much does the firm earn from these
> > investments and for how long?</span>
>
> <div class="calibre25">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> </div>

</div>

<div class="calibre53">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">The net effects of the capitalization will be
seen most visibly in the reinvestment rates and returns on capital you
estimate for these firms.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre6"><span class="bold">Intrinsic
Valuation</span></span>

<div class="calibre25">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">When you capitalize the expenses associated with
creating intangible assets, you are in effect redoing the financial
statements of the firm and restating numbers that are fundamental inputs
into valuation—earnings, reinvestment, and measures of returns.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

> <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Earnings:</span> Adding
> back the current year’s expense and subtracting out the amortization
> of past expenses, the effect on earnings will generally be positive if
> expenses have risen over time. With Amgen, for instance, where R&D
> expenses increased from \$663 million at the start of the amortization
> period to \$3.03 billion in the current year, the earnings increased
> by more than \$1.3 billion as a result of the R&D adjustment.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

> <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Reinvestment:</span> The
> effect on reinvestment is identical to the effect on earnings, with
> reinvestment increasing or decreasing by exactly the same amount as
> earnings. That will generally increase the reinvestment rate.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

> <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Capital invested:</span>
> Since the unamortized portion of the prior year’s expenses is treated
> as an asset, it adds to the estimated equity or capital invested in
> the firm. The effect will increase with the amortizable life and
> should therefore be higher for pharmaceutical firms (where amortizable
> lives tend to be longer) than for software firms (where research pays
> off far more quickly as commercial products).</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

> <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Return on equity
> (capital):</span> Since both earnings and capital invested are
> affected by capitalization, the net effects on return on equity and
> capital are unpredictable. If the return on equity (capital) increases
> after the recapitalization, it can be considered a rough indicator
> that the returns earned by the firm on its R&D is greater than its
> returns on traditional investments.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">In addition to providing us with more realistic
estimates of what these firms are investing in their growth and the
quality of their investments, the capitalization process also restores
consistency to valuations by ensuring that growth rates are in line with
reinvestment and return on capital assumptions. Thus, technology or
pharmaceutical firms that want to continue to grow have to keep
investing in R&D, while ensuring that these investments, at least
collectively, generate high returns for the firm.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">How much of an impact will capitalizing R&D have
on the value per share? To illustrate, we valued Amgen, using both the
unadjusted accounting numbers and the numbers adjusted for capitalized
R&D. The numbers are summarized in
<a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos335128"
id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos334973"
class="calibre7">Table 11.3</a>.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17"><span class="bold"><span id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos335128"
class="calibre36"></span><a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos334973"
class="calibre7">Table 11.3</a> Valuation Fundamentals: With and Without
R&D Capitalization</span></span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

| <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold"></span></span> | <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold">Conventional</span></span> | <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold">Capitalized R&D</span></span> |
|----|----|----|
| <span class="calibre30">After-tax ROC</span> | <span class="calibre30">14.91%</span> | <span class="calibre30">17.41%</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Reinvestment rate</span> | <span class="calibre30">19.79%</span> | <span class="calibre30">33.23%</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Growth rate</span> | <span class="calibre30">2.95%</span> | <span class="calibre30">5.78%</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Value per share</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$43.63</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$62.97</span> |

<div class="calibre51">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">The value per share would have been \$43.63 if
we had used conventional accounting numbers, about 10 percent under the
stock price at the time of \$47.97. With capitalized R&D, the value per
share is significantly higher and the stock looks cheap. In general, the
effect on value will be negative for firms that invest large amounts in
R&D, with little to show (yet) in terms of earnings and cash flows in
subsequent periods. It will be positive for firms that reinvest large
amounts in R&D and report large increases in earnings in subsequent
periods. In the case of Amgen, capitalizing R&D has a positive effect on
value per share, because of its track record of successful R&D.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre6"><span class="bold">Relative
Valuation</span></span>

<div class="calibre25">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre17">

It is true that all technology and pharmaceutical companies operate
under the same flawed accounting rules, expensing R&D rather than
capitalizing it. That does not mean, though, that there are no
consequences for relative valuation. The effect of capitalizing R&D on
earnings and book value can vary widely across firms and will depend
upon the following:

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

- <div class="calibre4">

   

  </div>

- <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Age of the firm and stage
  in life cycle:</span> Generally speaking, the proportional effects of
  capitalization on earnings and book value will be much greater at
  young growth firms than at more mature firms.</span>
  <div class="calibre4">

   

  </div>

  <div class="calibre4">

   

  </div>

- <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Amortizable life:</span>
  The effect of capitalizing expenses will be much greater as we extend
  the amortizable life of R&D, especially on capital invested. If
  different firms within the same business convert research into
  commercial products at different speeds, the effect on earnings of
  capitalizing R&D can vary across firms.</span>
  <div class="calibre4">

   

  </div>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

</div>

</p>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">If you ignore accounting inconsistencies and use
the reported earnings and book values of firms in the computation of
multiples, younger firms or firms that have R&D with longer gestation
periods will look overvalued (even if they are fairly priced or
bargains). Their earnings and book value will be understated, leading to
much higher PE, EV/EBITDA, and book value multiples for these
firms.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">There are two ways to incorporate these factors
into relative valuation. The first is to capitalize the expenses
associated with investing in intangible assets for each firm and to
compute consistent measures of earnings and book value to use in
multiples. This approach, while yielding the most precision, is also the
most time and data intensive. The second is to stick with the reported
accounting values for earnings and book value, while controlling for the
factors listed above.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17"><a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos339845"
id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos339302"
class="calibre7">Table 11.4</a> presents a relative valuation of
pharmaceutical firms, using three measures of PE ratios: the
conventional PE ratio (obtained by dividing the market capitalization by
the net income); a measure of PE computed using the sum of net income
and R&D; and an adjusted measure, where you net out the amortization of
R&D (Net income + R&D expense − Amortization of R&D).</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17"><span class="bold"><span id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos339845"
class="calibre36"></span><a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos339302"
class="calibre7">Table 11.4</a> Price/Earnings Ratios: Pharmaceutical
Companies</span></span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<img src="images/00066.jpg" class="calibre14" alt="image" />

<span class="calibre17">Astra Zeneca (AZN) looks undervalued, using
every measure of PE and Celgene (CELG) looks overvalued on all three
measures. Bristol Myers (BMY) looks overvalued on a conventional PE
ratio, slightly undervalued on the augmented earnings measure, and
correctly valued on the net R&D measure. We would argue that the last
measure, with both R&D and its amortization incorporated yields the
fairest comparison.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre6"><span class="bold">Dealing with Equity
Options</span></span>

<div class="calibre25">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">Firms that pay managers and others with equity
options are giving away some of the stockholders’ equity to these
people. To deal with the resulting loss in value to common stockholders,
there are three approaches that are employed in intrinsic valuation, and
we will use Google (GOOG), in early 2009, to illustrate all three. In
February 2009, we estimated a value for equity in the aggregate of
\$102,345 million for Google; the firm had 315.29 million shares
outstanding and 13.97 million in options outstanding, with an average
strike price of \$391.40/share.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre19">

> > <span class="calibre17">1. Assume that all or some of the options
> > will be exercised in the future, adjust the number of shares
> > outstanding and divide the value of equity by this number to arrive
> > at value per share; this is the diluted shares approach. To estimate
> > the value of equity per share in Google, divide the aggregate value
> > of equity estimated by the total number of shares outstanding,
> > including options.</span>
>
> > <span class="calibre17"><img src="images/00067.jpg" class="calibre14" alt="image" /></span>
>
> <div class="calibre4">
>
>  
>
> </div>

</div>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">While this approach has the virtue of
simplicity, it will lead to too low an estimate of value per share,
because it fails to reflect the proceeds from option exercise. In
Google’s case, each option that is exercised will bring in cash to the
firm.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre19">

> > <span class="calibre17">2. Incorporate the exercise proceeds from
> > the options in the numerator and then divide by the number of shares
> > that would be outstanding after exercise; this is the treasury stock
> > approach. Using this stock approach on Google:</span>
>
> > <span class="calibre17"><img src="images/00068.jpg" class="calibre14" alt="image" /></span>
>
> <div class="calibre4">
>
>  
>
> </div>

</div>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">This approach will yield too high a value per
share, largely because the approach ignores the time premium on the
option; an option trading at or out of the money may have no exercise
value but it still has option value.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre19">

> > <span class="calibre17">3. Estimate the value of the options today,
> > given today’s value per share and the time premium on the option.
> > Once this value has been estimated, it is subtracted from the
> > estimated equity value, and the remaining amount is divided by the
> > number of shares outstanding to arrive at value per share. Based
> > upon the exercise price (\$391.40) and the average maturity (3.50
> > years), the options outstanding at Google are valued at \$897
> > million and the resulting value per share is \$321.76.</span>
>
> > <span class="calibre17"><img src="images/00069.jpg" class="calibre14" alt="image" /></span>
>
> <div class="calibre4">
>
>  
>
> </div>

</div>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">When choosing which approach to take, consider
that the first is the crudest, the second is slightly more tempered, and
the third is the most work, but it is the right way to deal with
options. The fact that most investors and analysts do not go to the
trouble may provide an opportunity for those who go the extra mile in
assessing options.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">Comparing multiples across companies is
complicated by the fact that firms often have varying numbers of
employee options outstanding and these options can have very different
values. A failure to explicitly factor these options into analysis will
result in companies with unusually large or small (relative to the peer
group) numbers of options outstanding looking misvalued on a relative
basis. With PE ratios, for instance, using primary earnings per share
will make companies with more options outstanding look cheap. Using
fully diluted earnings per share will make firms that have long-term,
deep in-the-money options outstanding look cheap. The only way to
incorporate the effect of options into earnings multiples is to value
the options at fair value, using the current stock price as the basis,
and add this value on to the market capitalization to arrive at the
total market value of equity.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17"><a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos346050"
id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos345605"
class="calibre7">Table 11.5</a> summarizes a comparison of Google and
Cisco (CSCO) on a PE ratio basis, with different approaches for dealing
with options. While Cisco looks cheaper than Google, using all three
measures of PE ratios, it looks cheapest on a primary PE ratio basis and
less so with an option value approach.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17"><span class="bold"><span id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos346050"
class="calibre36"></span><a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_filepos345605"
class="calibre7">Table 11.5</a> Option Adjusted PE Ratios for Google and
Cisco</span></span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

| <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold"></span></span> | <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold">Google</span></span> | <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold">Cisco</span></span> |
|----|----|----|
| <span class="calibre30">Stock price</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$326.60</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$16.23</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Primary PE</span> | <span class="calibre30">24.37</span> | <span class="calibre30">11.04</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Diluted PE</span> | <span class="calibre30">25.45</span> | <span class="calibre30">13.25</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Market capitalization</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$102,975</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$97,153</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Value of options</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$1,406</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$3,477</span> |

| <span class="calibre30">Net Income after option expensing</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$4,227</span> | <span class="calibre30">\$8,052</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Adjusted PE</span> | <span class="calibre30">24.69</span> | <span class="calibre30">12.50</span> |

<div class="calibre51">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre55" data-border="1">

> <div class="calibre29">
>
> > <span class="calibre6"><span class="bold">Value Plays</span></span>
>
> <div class="calibre25">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> > <div class="calibre30">
> >
> > The biggest hurdle when investing in firms with intangible assets is
> > that the accounting numbers, at least as stated, are deceptive. As
> > an investor, you have to correct for these accounting problems and
> > focus on companies that have the following characteristics:
> >
> > <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > - <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> > - <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Intangible assets
> >   that generate high returns:</span> For intangible assets to
> >   generate value, they have to earn high returns. Look for a firm
> >   with intangible assets that are unique and difficult to
> >   replicate.</span>
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> > - <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Reasonable prices for
> >   “true” earnings:</span> Many firms with intangible assets have
> >   high growth potential and are priced to reflect that growth. Using
> >   the mismatch test, invest in companies that have high growth in
> >   earnings, corrected for accounting miscategorization, and that
> >   trade at low multiples of these corrected earnings.</span>
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> > - <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Spending to preserve
> >   and augment these intangible assets:</span> Intangible assets do
> >   not always stay valuable, especially if they are ignored. Focus on
> >   firms that invest in these assets (by spending on R&D, recruiting,
> >   or advertising) to preserve and grow value.</span>
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> > - <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Spending is
> >   efficient:</span> Not all expenditures on intangible assets
> >   generate value. Keep tabs on investments in intangible assets to
> >   see how quickly and how well they pay off and steer your money
> >   towards firms that rank highly on both dimensions.</span>
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> > - <span class="calibre17"><span class="italic">Equity claims drain
> >   per share value:</span> Firms with intangible assets tend to be
> >   big users of equity options as compensation, which can affect
> >   equity value per share. Incorporate the effects of outstanding
> >   options into your estimates of value per share and avoid companies
> >   that are cavalier about issuing new options to managers.</span>
> >   <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >    
> >
> >   </div>
> >
> > <div class="calibre4">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > </p>
>
> <div class="calibre25">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> > <span class="calibre30">In effect, you want to invest in companies
> > that make investments in intangible assets and are able to leverage
> > these assets to generate high returns, while protecting your share
> > of equity ownership.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre25">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> </div>

</div>

<div class="calibre53">

 

</div>

</div>

<div id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_020.html_calibre_pb_20"
class="mbppagebreak">

</div>

<span id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_021.html"></span>

<div id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_021.html_filepos350572"
class="calibre1">

<span class="calibre10"><span class="bold"><span class="italic">Conclusion</span></span></span>

<div class="calibre15">

 

</div>

Rules for the Road

<span class="calibre17">The more things change, the more they stay the
same. As we employed both intrinsic and relative valuation techniques to
value firms across the life cycle from Evergreen Solar, a young growth
company, to Sears, a company whose best days are behind it, we followed
a familiar script. The enduring theme is that value rests on standard
ingredients: cash flows, growth, and risk, though the effects of each
can vary across companies and across time.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre10"><span class="bold"><span class="calibre36"></span>Common
Ingredients</span></span>

<div class="calibre25">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">No matter what type of company you are valuing,
you have to decide whether you are valuing just equity or the entire
business, the approach you will use to estimate value (intrinsic versus
relative valuation), and the key components of value.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">When valuing a business, you can choose to value
the equity in the business or you can value the entire business. If you
value the business, you can get to the value of equity by adding back
assets that you have not valued yet (cash and cross holdings) and
subtracting out what you owe (debt). The choice matters because all of
your inputs—cash flows, growth, and risk—have to be defined
consistently. For most of the companies that we have valued in this
book, we have valued the businesses and backed into the value of equity.
With financial service firms, our inability to define debt and estimate
cash flows did push us into using equity valuation models.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">You can also value a business based on its
fundamentals, which is the intrinsic value, or you can value it by
looking at how the market prices similar firms in the market. While both
approaches yield estimates of value, they answer different questions.
With intrinsic valuation, the question we are answering is: Given this
company’s cash flows and risk, it is under- or overvalued? With relative
valuation, the question being answered is: Is this company cheap or
expensive, given how the market is pricing other companies just like
this one? With the example of Under Armour in Chapter 6, the intrinsic
valuation approach led us to conclude that the company was undervalued,
whereas the relative valuation would have led us to conclude that the
stock is overvalued.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">In both intrinsic and relative valuation, the
value of a company rests on three ingredients: cash flows from existing
assets, the expected growth in these cash flows, and the discount rate
that reflects the risk in those cash flows. In intrinsic valuation, we
are explicit about our estimates for these inputs. In relative
valuation, we try to control for differences across firms on these
inputs, when comparing how they are priced.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre10"><span class="bold"><span class="calibre36"></span>Differences
in Emphasis</span></span>

<div class="calibre25">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">The models and approaches used are identical for
all companies, but the choices we make and the emphasis we put on inputs
varied across companies. As illustrated in
<a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_021.html_filepos354472"
id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_021.html_filepos354175"
class="calibre7">Table 12.1</a>, the value drivers that were highlighted
in each chapter reflect the shifts in focus, as firms move through the
life cycle and across sectors.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17"><span class="bold"><a href="#The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_021.html_filepos354175"
id="The_Little_B-k_of_Valuation_split_021.html_filepos354472"
class="calibre7">Table 12.1</a> Value Drivers across the Life Cycle and
Sectors</span></span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

| <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold">Category</span></span> | <span class="calibre30"><span class="bold">Value Drivers</span></span> |
|----|----|
| <span class="calibre30">Young growth companies</span> | <span class="calibre30">Revenue growth, target margin, survival probability</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Growth companies</span> | <span class="calibre30">Scaling growth, margin sustainability</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Mature companies</span> | <span class="calibre30">Operating slack, financial slack, probability of management change</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Declining companies</span> | <span class="calibre30">Going concern value, default probability, default consequences</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Financial service firms</span> | <span class="calibre30">Equity risk, quality of growth (return on equity), regulatory capital buffers</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Commodity and cyclical companies</span> | <span class="calibre30">Normalized earnings, excess returns, Long-term growth</span> |
| <span class="calibre30">Intangible asset companies</span> | <span class="calibre30">Nature of intangible assets, efficiency of investments in intangible assets</span> |

<div class="calibre51">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">These value drivers are useful not only to
investors who want to determine what companies offer the best investment
odds, but also to managers in these firms, in terms of where they should
be focusing their attention to increase value.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre10"><span class="bold"><span class="calibre36"></span>And
the Payoff</span></span>

<div class="calibre25">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">Can you make money on your valuations? The
answer depends on three variables. The first is the quality of your
valuation. Well done valuations based upon better information should
generate better returns than shoddy valuations based upon rumor or
worse. The second is market feedback. To make money on even the
best-done valuation, the market has to correct its mistakes. The payoff
to valuation is likely to be speedier and more lucrative in smoothly
functioning markets. In more selfish terms, you want the market to be
efficient for the most part, with pockets of inefficiency that you can
exploit. The third and final factor is luck. While this will violate
your sense of fairness, luck can overwhelm good valuation skills. While
you cannot depend on good luck, you can reduce the impact of luck on
your returns by spreading your bets across many companies that you have
found to be undervalued. Diversification still pays!</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre10"><span class="bold"><span class="calibre36"></span>Parting
Words</span></span>

<div class="calibre25">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre17">Do not let experts and investment professionals
intimidate you. All too often, they are using the same information that
you are and their understanding of valuation is no deeper than yours. Do
not be afraid to make mistakes. I hope that even if not all of your
investments are winners, the process of analyzing investments and
assessing value brings you as much joy as it has brought me.</span>

<div class="calibre4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre55" data-border="1">

> <div class="calibre29">
>
> > <span class="calibre6"><span class="bold">10 Rules for the
> > Road</span></span>
>
> <div class="calibre25">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <div class="calibre19">
>
> > > <span class="calibre30">1. Feel free to abandon models, but do not
> > > budge on first principles.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">2. Pay heed to markets, but do not let
> > > them determine what you do.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">3. Risk affects value.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">4. Growth is not free and is not always
> > > good for value.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">5. All good things, including growth, come
> > > to an end. Nothing is forever.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">6. Watch out for truncation risk; many
> > > firms do not make it.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">7. Look at the past, but think about the
> > > future.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">8. Remember the law of large numbers. An
> > > average is better than a single number.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">9. Accept uncertainty, face up to it and
> > > deal with it.</span>
> >
> > <div class="calibre8">
> >
> >  
> >
> > </div>
> >
> > > <span class="calibre30">10. Convert stories to numbers.</span>
